Like at church, by the potluck table, I’ll just be holding the serving spoon up and not moving, or I’ll fold the same napkins again and again while folks are waiting.\n\nI don’t know why I do it. It’s not like when my sister used to fold sheets—she had a way about hospital corners, real particular. Mine’s not like that. It just happens, and then I notice people looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or images that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really images, no. I mean, I think about things, same as anybody. Like whether I turned the stove off or what I need from the store, and then I remember I don’t cook like I used to anyway. My daughter says I buy too much bread.\n\nBut repeating in my mind? I haven’t noticed that, not like a picture or a thought going round and round. It’s more… I’m just there, and then I realize I’ve been doing something too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you realize you've been doing something like folding napkins over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious… maybe embarrassed, mostly, because people are waiting and I’m just there with the napkins. Then I start thinking, oh Lord, they’re all seeing me do this.\n\nBut anxious… I’m not sure. My hands don’t shake or anything that I’ve noticed. It’s more like I come to and think, why am I still doing this? At church they used to have these paper napkins with flowers on them, and the kids would take too many for the lemonade table… but no, I don’t rightly know if it’s anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like folding the napkins or standing still, for some reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I have to, exactly. It’s not like I tell myself, “Do this or something bad will happen.” I just… I came in because at church I keep doing these odd things. Like I’ll be by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and I don’t move, or I’ll fold that same stack of napkins over and over while people are waiting on me.\n\nAnd then somebody says my name or clears their throat, and I realize I’m still standing there. It’s embarrassing. The ladies at church try to be kind, but you know how folks notice things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order when you're doing these repetitive actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… order, like straight lines and everything? I haven’t really thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll make the edges of the napkins match up, but that’s just how you fold them, isn’t it?\n\nI don’t feel like they have to be perfect or lined up a certain special way. I just get… stuck with it, I guess. My sister used to fuss if the table wasn’t set right on Sundays—fork on the wrong side and all that—but no, I can’t say I’ve noticed it needing to be in an order.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of getting stuck doing things, like folding napkins or standing still, interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, some. I don’t like to make a fuss about it, but I’ve had to step back from helping with the choir and some of the church volunteering.\n\nI used to help with little things, you know, passing out papers or setting up, making sure people knew where to go. But then I’d mix up plans, or folks would have to come behind me and redo what I was supposed to handle. And people get uncomfortable, even if they’re trying to be polite.\n\nIt’s hard, because church has always been… well, it’s where you see everybody, and the choir robes used to be kept in that little closet that smelled like dust and perfume. But yes, it’s gotten in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you cannot stop yourself from doing these things, like folding the napkins or standing still?\n\nPatient: It feels that way sometimes, yes… like I get caught in it before I notice. I’ll be there at church, by the potluck table, holding the serving spoon up like I’m about to serve somebody, and then I just don’t move. Or I’ll fold that same stack of napkins again and again while people are waiting.\n\nIt isn’t that I decide to keep doing it. It’s more like… I don’t stop until someone says something or I come back to myself. It makes me feel foolish, because everybody’s trying to get their plate and I’m just standing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel are being pushed into your mind repeatedly, even if they don't make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know what you mean by pushed in. Like a thought that comes in and won’t leave? I get little worries sometimes, but everybody does, don’t they?\n\nI haven’t noticed anything like a message being put there, no… not in that way. Sometimes a hymn will run through my head after choir, especially the old ones my mother liked, and then I’ll be thinking about whether I left the kettle on, and then I remember we had a blue kettle when I was a girl—\n\nBut no, I don’t think I have thoughts being shoved into my mind like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don't want to think about and can't seem to get rid of?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like something ugly or strange that I can’t push away. I mean, I worry about ordinary things, like whether I put something back where it belongs, or if I’ve forgotten what I came into the room for.\n\nSometimes a hymn or a line from church will keep going around in my head, but that happens after you sing it enough times, you know. My mother used to hum while she ironed, and I can still hear some of those old tunes if I get to thinking about her.\n\nBut thoughts I don’t want that just won’t leave… I don’t believe so. Not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that seem strange or don't make sense to you?\n\nPatient: Sometimes… I don’t know if the thoughts are strange, exactly, but when someone asks me something plain, like the ladies asking about blankets for the church room, I mean to answer them proper.\n\nThen my words just sort of wander off. I’ll start about the blankets, and then I’m talking about beans for the potluck, and then a hymn we used to sing, and then my roses out back. In my head it feels like it’s connected somehow, but people look at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nSo I suppose it doesn’t make sense to them… and then I get a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don't want to think about?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I don’t sit there fighting with a thought, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I remember things I’d rather not fuss over, like whether I said the wrong thing to someone at church, and then I’ll think about the flowers on the table there, and who arranged them, because Marjorie always did them so nice—\n\nBut no, I don’t think I have thoughts coming in that I don’t want to think about. Not in any big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you didn't intentionally think about and that seem unrelated to what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, maybe that’s more like what happens. I’ll be doing one thing, like trying to answer about the blankets for the church room, or choir practice, and I mean to say it plain. But then my words run off somewhere else.\n\nI’ll start with the blankets, and then I’m talking about beans for the supper, and then some hymn we sang years ago, and then my roses out back. It feels like it belongs together in my head, but folks look at me like they can’t find what I’m saying. That makes me feel foolish sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that are unwanted and disrupt what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not unwanted like I’m trying to push them away and they stop me. Sometimes I lose my place, you know, like I’m doing the dishes and then I remember I meant to put the kettle on, and then I see the little towel with the blue stitching and think of my aunt who used to make those—\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t noticed thoughts barging in and ruining what I’m doing, not exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these repetitive actions, like folding the napkins, in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “have to,” exactly… but yes, sometimes I get stuck with it. Like at church, I’ll be folding the napkins and then I fold the same little stack again and again, and people are waiting for me to put them out.\n\nAnd once I was by the potluck table holding up the serving spoon, just standing there, not moving. I knew folks were around me, and the casserole was getting cold probably—well, Dorothy makes that green bean one—but I just stayed like that. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you didn't ask for and that feel like they're interrupting what you're thinking or doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not like a thought jumps in and says, “stop that,” if that’s what you mean. My mind wanders, sure. I can be thinking about setting the table and then I’m remembering the curtains my sister had, little yellow flowers on them, and how she always kept them so clean.\n\nBut I don’t know that it feels like an interruption exactly. It’s more like I drift off the path before I notice I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must follow a specific routine or ritual when performing daily tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a routine like I plan it out, no… it’s more I get caught. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table with the serving spoon lifted up, and then I’m just standing there, not moving, while people are waiting.\n\nOr the napkins, I fold the same little stack over and over instead of putting them out. It feels odd after, when I notice everybody’s looking. I didn’t mean to make trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’m trying to stop something bad, not like that. It’s more that I just get stuck doing it. Like I told you, at church I’ll be there by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up, just standing there, and people are waiting on me. Or I fold the same napkins again and again instead of putting them out.\n\nI don’t have a reason in my head, really. It just happens, and then afterward I feel foolish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the napkins need to be folded in a specific way to look right when you're folding them over and over?\n\nPatient: Not really to look right, no… I don’t think I’m fixing them special. I just get going with them and then I’m still there, folding that same little stack over and over, and folks are waiting.\n\nThat’s why I came, really. At church I’ve been doing odd things like that… or standing by the potluck table with the serving spoon held up, not moving. I know it looks strange after. I don’t mean for it to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that it’s exactly the same way… I haven’t really noticed that part. I just know I’ll be handling them and then I’m still handling them, and time’s gone by.

Sometimes I start thinking about whether there’s enough coffee cups out, or if Mrs. Allen brought that casserole she makes, and then I look down and I’ve got the napkins in my hands again. So… no, I can’t say it has to be the same way each time. I’m not sure.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F68.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because people at church have been looking at me funny. At the potluck, I’ll be standing there by the table holding the serving spoon up, just not moving, and I don’t know why I’m doing it. And sometimes I fold the same stack of napkins over and over while folks are waiting on me.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I’ve been going to that church a long time, you know, they used to make the best casseroles, and Mrs. Hall always says the green beans need more salt… but anyway, that’s why I’m here. It’s like I get stuck doing odd things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to make things perfect or symmetrical when you're folding those napkins over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s about making them perfect. I’m not trying to get the corners just right or anything like that. It’s more like… I get stuck doing it. Like at church, by the potluck table, I’ll just be holding the serving spoon up and not moving, or I’ll fold the same napkins again and again while folks are waiting.\n\nI don’t know why I do it. It’s not like when my sister used to fold sheets—she had a way about hospital corners, real particular. Mine’s not like that. It just happens, and then I notice people looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or images that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really images, no. I mean, I think about things, same as anybody. Like whether I turned the stove off or what I need from the store, and then I remember I don’t cook like I used to anyway. My daughter says I buy too much bread.\n\nBut repeating in my mind? I haven’t noticed that, not like a picture or a thought going round and round. It’s more… I’m just there, and then I realize I’ve been doing something too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you realize you've been doing something like folding napkins over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious… maybe embarrassed, mostly, because people are waiting and I’m just there with the napkins. Then I start thinking, oh Lord, they’re all seeing me do this.\n\nBut anxious… I’m not sure. My hands don’t shake or anything that I’ve noticed. It’s more like I come to and think, why am I still doing this? At church they used to have these paper napkins with flowers on them, and the kids would take too many for the lemonade table… but no, I don’t rightly know if it’s anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like folding the napkins or standing still, for some reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I have to, exactly. It’s not like I tell myself, “Do this or something bad will happen.” I just… I came in because at church I keep doing these odd things. Like I’ll be by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and I don’t move, or I’ll fold that same stack of napkins over and over while people are waiting on me.\n\nAnd then somebody says my name or clears their throat, and I realize I’m still standing there. It’s embarrassing. The ladies at church try to be kind, but you know how folks notice things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order when you're doing these repetitive actions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… order, like straight lines and everything? I haven’t really thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll make the edges of the napkins match up, but that’s just how you fold them, isn’t it?\n\nI don’t feel like they have to be perfect or lined up a certain special way. I just get… stuck with it, I guess. My sister used to fuss if the table wasn’t set right on Sundays—fork on the wrong side and all that—but no, I can’t say I’ve noticed it needing to be in an order.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of getting stuck doing things, like folding napkins or standing still, interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, some. I don’t like to make a fuss about it, but I’ve had to step back from helping with the choir and some of the church volunteering.\n\nI used to help with little things, you know, passing out papers or setting up, making sure people knew where to go. But then I’d mix up plans, or folks would have to come behind me and redo what I was supposed to handle. And people get uncomfortable, even if they’re trying to be polite.\n\nIt’s hard, because church has always been… well, it’s where you see everybody, and the choir robes used to be kept in that little closet that smelled like dust and perfume. But yes, it’s gotten in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you cannot stop yourself from doing these things, like folding the napkins or standing still?\n\nPatient: It feels that way sometimes, yes… like I get caught in it before I notice. I’ll be there at church, by the potluck table, holding the serving spoon up like I’m about to serve somebody, and then I just don’t move. Or I’ll fold that same stack of napkins again and again while people are waiting.\n\nIt isn’t that I decide to keep doing it. It’s more like… I don’t stop until someone says something or I come back to myself. It makes me feel foolish, because everybody’s trying to get their plate and I’m just standing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel are being pushed into your mind repeatedly, even if they don't make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know what you mean by pushed in. Like a thought that comes in and won’t leave? I get little worries sometimes, but everybody does, don’t they?\n\nI haven’t noticed anything like a message being put there, no… not in that way. Sometimes a hymn will run through my head after choir, especially the old ones my mother liked, and then I’ll be thinking about whether I left the kettle on, and then I remember we had a blue kettle when I was a girl—\n\nBut no, I don’t think I have thoughts being shoved into my mind like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don't want to think about and can't seem to get rid of?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like something ugly or strange that I can’t push away. I mean, I worry about ordinary things, like whether I put something back where it belongs, or if I’ve forgotten what I came into the room for.\n\nSometimes a hymn or a line from church will keep going around in my head, but that happens after you sing it enough times, you know. My mother used to hum while she ironed, and I can still hear some of those old tunes if I get to thinking about her.\n\nBut thoughts I don’t want that just won’t leave… I don’t believe so. Not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that seem strange or don't make sense to you?\n\nPatient: Sometimes… I don’t know if the thoughts are strange, exactly, but when someone asks me something plain, like the ladies asking about blankets for the church room, I mean to answer them proper.\n\nThen my words just sort of wander off. I’ll start about the blankets, and then I’m talking about beans for the potluck, and then a hymn we used to sing, and then my roses out back. In my head it feels like it’s connected somehow, but people look at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nSo I suppose it doesn’t make sense to them… and then I get a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don't want to think about?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I don’t sit there fighting with a thought, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I remember things I’d rather not fuss over, like whether I said the wrong thing to someone at church, and then I’ll think about the flowers on the table there, and who arranged them, because Marjorie always did them so nice—\n\nBut no, I don’t think I have thoughts coming in that I don’t want to think about. Not in any big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you didn't intentionally think about and that seem unrelated to what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, maybe that’s more like what happens. I’ll be doing one thing, like trying to answer about the blankets for the church room, or choir practice, and I mean to say it plain. But then my words run off somewhere else.\n\nI’ll start with the blankets, and then I’m talking about beans for the supper, and then some hymn we sang years ago, and then my roses out back. It feels like it belongs together in my head, but folks look at me like they can’t find what I’m saying. That makes me feel foolish sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that are unwanted and disrupt what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not unwanted like I’m trying to push them away and they stop me. Sometimes I lose my place, you know, like I’m doing the dishes and then I remember I meant to put the kettle on, and then I see the little towel with the blue stitching and think of my aunt who used to make those—\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t noticed thoughts barging in and ruining what I’m doing, not exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these repetitive actions, like folding the napkins, in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “have to,” exactly… but yes, sometimes I get stuck with it. Like at church, I’ll be folding the napkins and then I fold the same little stack again and again, and people are waiting for me to put them out.\n\nAnd once I was by the potluck table holding up the serving spoon, just standing there, not moving. I knew folks were around me, and the casserole was getting cold probably—well, Dorothy makes that green bean one—but I just stayed like that. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you didn't ask for and that feel like they're interrupting what you're thinking or doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not like a thought jumps in and says, “stop that,” if that’s what you mean. My mind wanders, sure. I can be thinking about setting the table and then I’m remembering the curtains my sister had, little yellow flowers on them, and how she always kept them so clean.\n\nBut I don’t know that it feels like an interruption exactly. It’s more like I drift off the path before I notice I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must follow a specific routine or ritual when performing daily tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a routine like I plan it out, no… it’s more I get caught. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table with the serving spoon lifted up, and then I’m just standing there, not moving, while people are waiting.\n\nOr the napkins, I fold the same little stack over and over instead of putting them out. It feels odd after, when I notice everybody’s looking. I didn’t mean to make trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’m trying to stop something bad, not like that. It’s more that I just get stuck doing it. Like I told you, at church I’ll be there by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up, just standing there, and people are waiting on me. Or I fold the same napkins again and again instead of putting them out.\n\nI don’t have a reason in my head, really. It just happens, and then afterward I feel foolish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the napkins need to be folded in a specific way to look right when you're folding them over and over?\n\nPatient: Not really to look right, no… I don’t think I’m fixing them special. I just get going with them and then I’m still there, folding that same little stack over and over, and folks are waiting.\n\nThat’s why I came, really. At church I’ve been doing odd things like that… or standing by the potluck table with the serving spoon held up, not moving. I know it looks strange after. I don’t mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you repeat, like folding the napkins, need to be done exactly the same way each time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s exactly the same way… I haven’t really noticed that part. I just know I’ll be handling them and then I’m still handling them, and time’s gone by.\n\nSometimes I start thinking about whether there’s enough coffee cups out, or if Mrs. Allen brought that casserole she makes, and then I look down and I’ve got the napkins in my hands again. So… no, I can’t say it has to be the same way each time. I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I haven’t noticed thoughts barging in and ruining what I’m doing, not exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these repetitive actions, like folding the napkins, in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “have to,” exactly… but yes, sometimes I get stuck with it. Like at church, I’ll be folding the napkins and then I fold the same little stack again and again, and people are waiting for me to put them out.\n\nAnd once I was by the potluck table holding up the serving spoon, just standing there, not moving. I knew folks were around me, and the casserole was getting cold probably—well, Dorothy makes that green bean one—but I just stayed like that. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you didn't ask for and that feel like they're interrupting what you're thinking or doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not like a thought jumps in and says, “stop that,” if that’s what you mean. My mind wanders, sure. I can be thinking about setting the table and then I’m remembering the curtains my sister had, little yellow flowers on them, and how she always kept them so clean.\n\nBut I don’t know that it feels like an interruption exactly. It’s more like I drift off the path before I notice I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must follow a specific routine or ritual when performing daily tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a routine like I plan it out, no… it’s more I get caught. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table with the serving spoon lifted up, and then I’m just standing there, not moving, while people are waiting.\n\nOr the napkins, I fold the same little stack over and over instead of putting them out. It feels odd after, when I notice everybody’s looking. I didn’t mean to make trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’m trying to stop something bad, not like that. It’s more that I just get stuck doing it. Like I told you, at church I’ll be there by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up, just standing there, and people are waiting on me. Or I fold the same napkins again and again instead of putting them out.\n\nI don’t have a reason in my head, really. It just happens, and then afterward I feel foolish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the napkins need to be folded in a specific way to look right when you're folding them over and over?\n\nPatient: Not really to look right, no… I don’t think I’m fixing them special. I just get going with them and then I’m still there, folding that same little stack over and over, and folks are waiting.\n\nThat’s why I came, really. At church I’ve been doing odd things like that… or standing by the potluck table with the serving spoon held up, not moving. I know it looks strange after. I don’t mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you repeat, like folding the napkins, need to be done exactly the same way each time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s exactly the same way… I haven’t really noticed that part. I just know I’ll be handling them and then I’m still handling them, and time’s gone by.\n\nSometimes I start thinking about whether there’s enough coffee cups out, or if Mrs. Allen brought that casserole she makes, and then I look down and I’ve got the napkins in my hands again. So… no, I can’t say it has to be the same way each time. I’m not sure."
